English Script — Full Narration (L1–L17)

L1 "STEAM = Elite Science Education" Is a Misconception (approx. 5 min)
Introduction

Hi everyone! I'm Reiko.

Thank you so much for taking this course.

In our course on non-cognitive skills and the SMILE method, we explored how the abilities you can't measure with test scores — things like empathy, curiosity, and grit — are the real foundation for a child's future. Today, we're going to take that one step further and dive into STEAM education.

Have you heard of STEAM education before?

Some of you might be thinking, "Isn't that just elite science education?"

"Like... enrolling your children in coding classes?"

"My child is more of more into arts and humanities, so it probably doesn't apply to us."

If that sounds like you — you're not alone.

Or maybe you're thinking, "That's something for when they start school, right? It's too early for young children."

Well, here's the thing — all of that is actually a misconception.

STEAM education is not about drilling specific subjects into your child. And it's not something only for children with special talents.

It's actually a very accessible approach to learning that's relevant to every single child.

What Is STEAM?

STEAM stands for five fields:

S — Science
T — Technology
E — Engineering
A — Arts
M — Mathematics

It originally started as STEM — without the "A" — in the United States, as an initiative to develop talent in science and technology fields.

In the 2000s, there was a growing concern that there weren't enough people going into STEM careers, and that's what kicked it all off.

But along the way, people realized something important.

Science and technology alone don't create innovation.

You've probably heard the famous story about Steve Jobs and calligraphy, right? He sat in on a calligraphy class — the art of beautiful lettering — and was deeply moved by it. That experience later inspired the beautiful fonts on the Mac.

Even after dropping out of college, Jobs kept attending that one calligraphy class because it fascinated him.

The beauty of the letters, the thickness of the lines, the balance and spacing.

That sense of art he felt in that classroom ended up fundamentally changing the world of computers.

In other words, real creativity is born where science meets art.

And that's how the "A" — Arts — was added, turning STEM into STEAM.

The True Essence of STEAM

Now, here's the important part. STEAM is not about teaching five separate subjects.

The true essence of STEAM is this cycle:

Wonder — "Why?"
Hypothesize — "What if...?"
Try it out.
Discover.

It's the cycle of inquiry itself.

And doesn't that sound exactly like what children do every single day?

Throwing a stone into a puddle and watching the ripples spread.

Stacking blocks as high as they can and laughing when they topple over.

Splashing their hands in the bathtub to see how the water flies.

That — right there — is STEAM.

Children are born scientists.

What we need to do, as the adults in their lives, is simply not crush that natural drive.

You don't need fancy materials. You don't need expensive coding classes.

STEAM Is a Global Movement

Governments around the world are now officially promoting STEAM education. Countries everywhere are moving in this direction.

And that's exactly why I want to use this course to show you how you can bring STEAM into your home.

You don't need a special environment. You can start today, right in the small world of your own family.

That's the beauty of STEAM education.

By the time you finish this course, you'll be able to watch your child playing in the park and think, "Oh — they're doing STEAM right now."

You'll start to see so many learning moments hidden in everyday life.

So — let's explore the world of STEAM education together!

L2 Why "The Power to Think" Becomes Your Greatest Weapon in the Age of AI (approx. 5 min)
The Age of AI

As we touched on when talking about non-cognitive skills, we're now living in an era where ChatGPT can solve entrance exams for the world's top universities.

In a time when AI can hold all the knowledge for us — have you ever stopped and wondered: what can we, as parents, actually leave our children?

Have you ever thought about this?

"By the time my child grows up, half the jobs that exist today might be gone."

Research from Oxford University has pointed out that within the next 10 to 20 years, about 47% of jobs could be replaced by AI.

In such an uncertain world, what can we prepare for our children?

One of the answers is STEAM education.

What AI Can and Cannot Do

AI is great at answering questions that have a clear, correct answer.

It can process massive amounts of data and find the optimal solution.

It can translate. It can write. It can even draw.

But there's something AI struggles with.

Creating the question itself.

"Why is the sky blue?"

"If there were no gravity, what kind of house would we live in?"

"How could we make this town more fun?"

The ability to ask those fundamental questions — that's exactly the kind of power STEAM education develops.

As I also shared in my book JOY-katsu, AI may be great at memory and analysis, but it cannot create something entirely new from nothing.

Feeling the world through your senses. Resonating with something in your heart. And then imagining something from that feeling.

These are things only humans can do.

What Silicon Valley Parents Know

I've been running a preschool in Silicon Valley for 18 years, and there's something I've really come to feel deeply.

Children of engineers from Google and Apple — many of them attended our school.

And their parents all said the same thing: "The ability to ask questions matters more than the ability to memorize answers."

Why? Because in the world of technology, the right answer changes constantly.

Last year's right answer becomes this year's wrong answer.

The people who survive in that world are the ones who can ask their own questions, test things out for themselves, and make their own discoveries.

One father, a Google engineer, put it this way: "The most important part of my job is finding problems no one else has noticed yet. AI can find answers — but only humans can ask the right questions."

Another thing that really stuck with me was what a mother who works at Apple said: "I don't want my child to just use technology — I want them to be the one creating it. And for that, the most important thing is nurturing the ability to think for yourself from a young age."

What these parents — people working at the cutting edge of technology — want for their children is not knowledge. It's the power to think.

That's really telling, isn't it?

The Power of Asking Questions

Here's another important perspective.

At my preschool, Sora, we place enormous value on the power of asking questions.

A child who can ask, "What do I want to do?" and "How can I make it happen?" — that child can carve out their own path in any environment.

STEAM education is the ultimate way to nurture this questioning ability.

"Why?" "How come?" "What if...?"

The ability to freely ask these kinds of questions is a uniquely human weapon that AI simply cannot replace.

Conclusion

STEAM education is not about raising smart children.

It's a way of thinking that raises children who can think for themselves.

And the best part? You don't need any special talent. You can nurture it right at home.

As I shared in JOY-katsu, drawing out that feeling of "I want to try!" and turning those ideas into real experiences — that is the starting point of STEAM education.

A child's "Why?" is a precious sprout of thinking power.

Nurturing that sprout is the greatest preparation you can give them for thriving in the age of AI.

In the next section, we'll look at the scientific evidence for why STEAM experiences during early childhood are so powerful for brain development.

L3 Your Child's Brain Is Growing the Most When They're Playing in the Sandbox (approx. 6 min)
Introduction

"My child just keeps playing in the sandbox all day long... Should I be making them study more?"

Have you ever felt this kind of worry?

Your friend's child is already writing letters. Another one has started math drills.

When you hear things like that, it's natural to panic a little and think, "Is my child going to be okay?"

But don't worry.

The truth is, from a brain science perspective, during that time your child is playing in the sandbox, their brain is working incredibly hard.

The Brain's Critical Window

The human brain is said to be approximately 90% formed by the age of six.

What makes the brain so special during this period is that it has extremely high neuroplasticity.

That's a fancy term, but in simple words, it means the brain is soft like a sponge, with an incredible ability to absorb experiences.

Of course, the adult brain changes too, but the flexibility of a young child's brain is on a completely different level.

What Happens in the Sandbox

When a child is building a mountain in the sandbox, what's going on inside their brain?

"How can I keep it from collapsing?" — They're forming a hypothesis.

"What if I pour water on it? Will it harden?" — They're running an experiment.

"Oh, the tunnel is open!" — They're making a discovery.

"Let me try to make it even taller!" — They're taking on a new challenge.

Through this entire process, the neurons — the nerve cells — in the brain are connecting with each other.

These connections are called synapses.

According to research from Harvard University's Center on the Developing Child, during early childhood, more than one million new synapses are formed every single second.

One million per second. That is a truly astonishing number.

And the synapses that get used often become stronger, while the ones that don't get used disappear.

This is called synaptic pruning.

The brain prunes itself to work more efficiently — it strengthens the circuits that are used frequently and trims away the ones that aren't.

In other words, what a child experiences directly shapes the wiring of their brain.

The time they spend playing in the sandbox is literally the time they're building their brain's circuitry.

A Real Example from Our School

Let me give you a specific example.

This happened during water play at our school.

A three-year-old boy was pouring water from one cup to another.

When he poured from the big cup into the small cup, the water overflowed.

When he poured from the small cup into the big cup, there was still room.

He repeated this dozens of times.

From the outside, it just looks like he's playing with water, right?

But inside this child's brain, he was simultaneously learning about the concept of volume, comparing capacity, understanding the properties of liquid — so many things all at once.

And nobody taught him any of it.

Why Exploration Beats Drills

Here's the important point. Compared to stimulation that's given to children — like flashcards or drills — experiences where children touch things themselves, try things out, and make their own discoveries create far richer synapse formation.

Why? Because during exploratory experiences, multiple regions of the brain are activated at the same time.

Touching with their hands. Observing with their eyes. Thinking "Why?" Feeling the joy of "I did it!"

All of this happens at once.

With drills, only limited areas of the brain are engaged.

But when a child is playing in the sandbox, vision, touch, thinking, emotion, movement — the entire brain is working simultaneously.

That's exactly why such rich synaptic networks are formed.

As I shared in my book JOY-katsu, the five senses are antennae that feel the world before thought even kicks in.

Touching mud. Reacting to sounds. Laughing in the wind.

Children are naturally masters of the five senses.

They live fully through their senses every day — something adults tend to forget how to do.

These exploratory experiences that engage all five senses are the very best nourishment for brain development.

Protecting That Precious Time

That's why it's so important for adults to be mindful not to take that time away from children.

We don't want to let overscheduling or an obsession with efficiency rob children of the time they need to be immersed in their senses.

The time your child spends completely absorbed in sandbox play is the ultimate brain training session.

So when your child is deeply engaged in something, rather than saying "Come on, let's go" and cutting it short right away, try to protect that time as much as you can.

That moment of total absorption — that's exactly when one million synapses per second are being formed most actively.

Conclusion

The foundation of STEAM education lies in this kind of brain growth through experience.

When your child is completely absorbed in something, try thinking to yourself: "Right now, my child's brain is growing at an incredible speed."

I bet you'll find that sight even more precious and lovable.

In the next lecture, we'll talk about another crucial brain function: executive function.

L4 Executive Function — The Brain's Command Center That Shapes Your Child's Future (approx. 6 min)
Introduction

Does your child ever do things like this?

They can't put their toys away.

They can't wait for their turn.

They lose interest quickly.

They can't hold back from doing what they want.

Do you think this is just a personality thing?

You might find yourself thinking, "My child is just sloppy," or "My child just can't sit still."

But the truth is, all of these are related to a brain function called executive function.

It's not about personality — it's about the stage of brain development.

What Is Executive Function?

Executive function is like the command center of the brain.

It's centered in the prefrontal cortex, and it controls our thinking and behavior.

Specifically, there are three key abilities.

1. Working Memory

This is the ability to temporarily hold information in your mind.

Remembering what Mom or Dad told you and acting on it.

For example, "Wash your hands, dry them with a towel, and sit down at the table." Remembering those three instructions in order and carrying them out.

That's working memory in action.

2. Inhibitory Control

This is the ability to hold back impulses.

"I want to eat right now, but I'll wait until everyone is ready."

"There's cake right in front of me, but I can wait until everyone is ready."

That's because inhibitory control is at work.

3. Cognitive Flexibility

This is the ability to shift your thinking.

"If this way doesn't work, let me try a different approach."

It's the ability to not get stuck on one method, but to flexibly think, "Well, how about this instead?"

Why Executive Function Matters More Than Test Scores

A research team at Harvard University has stated that executive function is the single strongest predictor of future success — even more so than academic ability.

In other words, the ability to control yourself matters far more for life than test scores.

Adele Diamond, a leading researcher in developmental cognitive neuroscience whom I also introduced in my book JOY-katsu, strongly advocates for the importance of executive function.

Executive function is the ability to control your emotions and to think ahead before acting.

As this ability develops, children build the power to control themselves — what we call self-regulation.

STEAM as the Ultimate Training Ground

And here's the key point: STEAM-based exploratory experiences are the ultimate training for executive function.

For example, building a bridge with blocks.

"Will it be stronger if I do it this way?"

"Oh, it collapsed. Let me try again."

"What if I make this part thicker?"

While they're playing, the brain's command center is working at full capacity.

Let me give you another example.

Helping out in the kitchen is incredibly effective too.

"Crack two eggs, pour in the milk, and mix it up."

Within this short set of instructions, you have working memory — remembering the steps. Inhibitory control — adjusting the force when cracking an egg. Cognitive flexibility — trying a different method when the egg doesn't crack cleanly.

It's all in there.

On the other hand, if adults just give orders — "Do it this way" — and the child simply follows along, executive function doesn't get much of a workout.

Thinking for yourself. Deciding for yourself. Trying things for yourself.

That "for yourself" part is the key to developing executive function.

The Connection to Self-Regulation

As I also shared in my book JOY-katsu, self-regulation and executive function are deeply connected.

The ability to do what needs to be done when it needs to be done — that self-regulation is built on the foundation of executive function.

By quietly repeating small habits in daily life, the power of self-regulation is gradually nurtured.

STEAM-based exploratory experiences are the best way to build that self-regulation while having fun.

Watch and Wait

Rather than telling your child, "Be patient!" or "Behave!" — letting them naturally strengthen their executive function through exploratory play is far more effective.

Saying "Be patient!" a hundred times is less effective for executive function than giving your child one experience of building a bridge with blocks.

And the most important thing is this: when things don't go well, don't rush in to help. Saying, "Oh, it fell down. What do you think we should do?" and simply watching over them — that is the very best support for developing executive function.

Conclusion

Executive function doesn't develop overnight.

But through daily STEAM-based play, it grows stronger little by little, steadily.

Don't rush it. Enjoy the process. That's the most important thing.

When your child is working persistently on something, all you need to say is, "You're almost there!"

That attitude of watching over them — that is the greatest support you can give for building executive function.

This is exactly why STEAM education is backed by brain science.

L5 Don't Teach the Answer — Nurture the Process of Scientific Thinking (approx. 4 min)
Introduction

"Mom, why is the sky blue?"

When your child asks you this, how do you respond?

Most people try to give the correct answer — something about light scattering.

Or maybe you're at a loss, thinking, "That's a tough one."

Some of you might look it up on your phone and say, "Apparently it's because of this."

But in STEAM education, giving the correct answer comes last.

Why? Because when you give the answer first, the entire thinking process in your child's mind gets skipped.

And it's actually that process that matters the most.

The Five Steps of Scientific Thinking

In the Science part of STEAM, the most important thing is to let children experience the process of scientific thinking.

Here's what that process looks like:

1. Observe. "Huh, that's strange."

2. Ask a question. "Why is that?"

3. Form a hypothesis. "Maybe it's because of..."

4. Experiment. "Let's try it and find out."

5. Discover. "Wow, look what happened!"

What matters is that the child goes through these five steps on their own.

When your child asks, "Why is the sky blue?" — just say, "You're right, why is it blue? What do you think, sweetie?"

That's all it takes for the child to start thinking on their own.

"Maybe the yellow of the sun mixes in and turns it blue?"

Even if they're wrong, that's completely fine.

In fact, forming an incorrect hypothesis is itself the starting point of scientific thinking.

What matters is not arriving at the correct answer — it's the process of having tried to think for themselves.

As they repeat this process, a habit of thinking takes root inside the child.

Don't Deny Their Ideas

Here's another very important point.

When your child's hypothesis is wrong, don't say "That's not right."

Instead, say something like, "That's an interesting idea! Shall we test it and find out?"

If their mistakes are denied, children will stop forming hypotheses altogether.

But if they feel their ideas are accepted as interesting, they become freer to think and explore.

The Power of Thinking for Yourself

As I shared in my book JOY-katsu, rather than producing correct answers to problems given to you, the ability to ask yourself what you want to do, figure out how to do it, try things, and get creative — that is a uniquely human quality that AI simply cannot replicate. And that is the power to thrive in the future.

The time you spend together with your child wondering "Why?" is the ultimate learning experience for your child — and it's actually a time of fresh discovery for parents, too.

Children's perspectives give us a chance to revisit the things adults have stopped questioning.

Conclusion

When your child asks "Why?" — that's your golden opportunity.

"Great question! What do you think, sweetie?"

With just that one phrase, you've planted the seeds of scientific thinking.

When you're busy and your child keeps asking "Why?" over and over, honestly, it can be exhausting sometimes.

You don't have to give a thoughtful answer to every single one.

But even just once a day, try saying, "Let's think about that together," or "Let's try it and see."

The key is: don't teach. And be amazed together.

Even if you already know the answer, wonder and marvel alongside your child. Say, "Wow, that's amazing!"

That sense of shared excitement is what lights the fire of your child's curiosity. That alone is enough to awaken the little scientist inside your child.

L6 Technology and Engineering Are Already in Your Living Room (approx. 6 min)
Introduction

When you hear "Technology and Engineering," you might think, "Oh, that's about computers and robots, right?"

And at the advanced level, yes, that's true.

But for children, the T and E in STEAM are found in much more familiar places.

The truth is, there's a lot of technological thinking and engineering thinking hidden in the things your child does every single day.

Technology: Using Tools to Solve Problems

The essence of technology is using tools to solve problems.

For young children, a spoon, scissors, and tape are technology.

"I want to reach something up high." So they drag a chair over and use it as a step stool. That is a perfectly valid example of technological thinking.

Using the tools at hand to figure out a way to solve the problem right in front of you — that is the very origin of technology.

When your child is trying to eat with a spoon on their own, that's already the beginning of their encounter with technology.

At first, they can't scoop properly and keep spilling.

But then they try changing how they hold it, adjusting the angle. They're teaching themselves how to use the tool.

Engineering: Build, Assemble, Improve

The essence of engineering is to build, to assemble, and to improve.

Building a secret fort out of cardboard boxes.

Making a sword out of newspaper.

Creating a waterwheel from a plastic bottle.

All of this is engineering.

And here's what's really important: the experience of things not working out is the real treasure.

The cardboard house collapsed.

"The roof was too heavy. Let me make the pillars thicker."

This cycle of failure, analyzing the cause, and improving — that is the core of engineering thinking.

The children at our school often do projects where they build houses out of cardboard. At first, the structures collapse right away. But then the children start thinking, "Why did it fall down?" They figure out on their own — "There wasn't enough tape," "This part was weak" — and they make improvements. That process is engineering itself.

The Kitchen: The Ultimate STEAM Lab

The kitchen is the ultimate STEAM lab.

Cracking an egg — that's the engineering of force control.

Water coming to a boil — that's the science of temperature change.

Using a timer — that's technology through tool use.

Measuring ingredients — that's mathematics.

For example, try making pancakes with your child.

Measure the flour and water.

Choose a tool for mixing.

Watch and ask, "How long should we cook it before we flip it?"

Use a spatula to flip it over.

Within a single recipe, every element of STEAM is packed in.

You don't need any special tools. Everyday life is your laboratory.

STEAM Is Everywhere at Home

You can do the same thing in the living room.

Folding laundry is actually engineering.

"How can I fold this neatly?"

"Why do we fold T-shirts and pants differently?"

Questions like these turn everyday moments into STEAM.

The bathtub is also a wonderful STEAM lab.

"The shampoo bottle sinks, but the cap floats — why is that?"

"Why does the mirror fog up when we add hot water?"

Try these little experiments with your child during bath time.

You Don't Need Special Materials

The T and E in STEAM are things children can naturally engage with in daily life — even without special materials or programming classes.

As I shared in my book, you don't need special knowledge or a special environment. Learning can begin right in the small world of your home.

The kitchen, the living room, the bathtub, the backyard. Your entire home is a STEAM lab.

What matters most is this: when your child wants to build something or fix something, don't jump straight to the answer. Instead, make time to explore and experiment together.

I know it's tempting to say "Try it this way" right away.

But hold back for just a moment, and ask, "What do you want to do, sweetie?" Those five seconds of patience are what nurture your child's engineering thinking.

And when things go well, celebrate together. When they don't, think together: "So what should we try next?"

This repetition is what steadily builds the power of T and E.

Please try just one home experiment with your child today.

You can start in the kitchen, the bathtub — anywhere at all.

L7 Arts — Where Children Express What Lives Inside Them (approx. 7 min)
Introduction

The A in STEAM — Arts. The Arts we're talking about here doesn't just mean drawing and music.

It refers to the liberal arts — the disciplines that set the human mind free.

Philosophy, history, literature, design, dance. Everything that involves humans feeling and expressing — all of that is Arts.

But some of you might be thinking, "My child isn't good at drawing, so..."

That's perfectly fine. Arts is not about drawing well.

It's about bringing what's inside of you to the outside. That is the essence of Arts.

Why Science and Technology Need Art

Why is art necessary in science and technology education?

The answer is simple. Science is the power to find the right answer. Art is the power to create an answer that doesn't exist yet.

When these two come together, that's when innovation is born.

As I shared in my book JOY-katsu, AI may be great at memory and analysis, but it cannot create something entirely new from nothing.

That's exactly why the power to imagine — to freely create in your mind — is what the future demands.

And that's why Arts is essential.

The Brain Science Behind Art

From a brain science perspective, there's something fascinating going on.

During art activities — drawing, playing with clay, making music — the corpus callosum, which connects the right and left hemispheres of the brain, becomes highly active.

In other words, art serves as a bridge between the creative right brain and the logical left brain.

This is an extremely important point.

If you only engage in scientific thinking, the left brain dominates. If you only engage in artistic thinking, the right brain dominates. But when STEAM education integrates the two, it enables rich thinking that uses the entire brain.

Research has also shown that children who are frequently exposed to artistic activities demonstrate higher problem-solving ability and critical thinking skills compared to those who are not.

Art isn't just fun — it's also a powerful tool for training the brain.

The Power of No Right Answer

And art has another great power.

There is no right answer.

In math, there are correct answers. But in a drawing, there is no correct answer.

The experience of freely expressing yourself in a world with no right answer — that is what builds the confidence inside a child that says, "My own ideas are good enough."

Stories from Our School

At our school, we have art time.

But we never say, "Draw this."

It's a time when children choose their own materials and bring their own ideas to life.

One day, a three-year-old girl painted a picture that was entirely black.

When her teacher asked, "What is this?" she said, "It's the nighttime sky. But you know what? There's a tiny star in here. Can you see it?"

When you looked closely, there was a faint white dot beneath the black paint.

This child was expressing the idea that there is light within the darkness.

If someone had said, "Paint something brighter," that expression would never have been born.

On another day, a four-year-old boy spent a long time making something out of clay.

It looked like a flat disc.

"What are you making?" his teacher asked. "The beginning of the universe. At first, everything was totally flat," he said.

In his own way, he was imagining and expressing the beginning of the universe.

What incredible imagination, right?

The Adult's Role: Don't Judge

When it comes to art, what adults should do is: don't judge.

Instead of "good" or "bad," ask: "How did you feel?" and "What did you want to express?"

Conclusion

Arts is the practice of children expressing what lives inside them.

The ability to give shape to what you feel — that is a power that holds up in any era, in any country.

When your child draws something or makes something, instead of saying "That's so good!" try asking, "What were you feeling when you made this? Tell me about it."

That one phrase can be the spark that makes your child's creativity blossom.

By the way, art activities at home aren't limited to drawing.

Dancing, singing, making up stories, shaping things out of clay, making a collage out of fallen leaves and acorns. The ways to express are infinite.

Find the form of expression that your child enjoys the most.

And please remember this: in art, the value isn't in the finished piece — it's in the time spent creating it.

A child who can enjoy the process builds a strong heart that doesn't get swept up by the outcome.

That, too, is something important that Arts teaches us.

Try putting your child's drawing on the fridge. Just that simple act sends the message: "What you expressed matters."

L8 Mathematics Is Not About Drills — It's About Discovering Patterns in the World (approx. 7 min)
Introduction

Do you think there really are children who are "bad at math"?

My answer is: in most cases, they're not actually bad at it — they just encountered it the wrong way.

In many cases, the reason children feel they're bad at math is that they started with calculation drills right from the beginning.

Before facing numbers on paper, it's important to first build a mathematical sense through hands-on experience.

Mathematics for Young Children

The M in STEAM — Mathematics.

For young children, math is not about calculation drills.

It's about finding patterns.

Lining up fallen leaves from biggest to smallest.

Sorting blocks by color.

Counting "one, two, three" while handing out snacks.

All of these are gateways to mathematical thinking.

On a walk, try pointing out: red flower, white flower, red flower, white flower. Then ask, "What color comes next?"

If your child says "red" — they're already engaging in pattern recognition, which is mathematical thinking.

Spatial Awareness

Something especially worth paying attention to is spatial awareness.

"Will this toy fit inside this box?"

The ability to picture that in your mind doesn't just become the foundation for geometry and physics later on — it comes alive in all sorts of fields like architecture, design, and programming.

Building with blocks is the ultimate tool for developing spatial awareness.

Which shape should go in which direction to stay stable?

How can I stack them higher?

Through play, children develop an intuitive understanding of three-dimensional space.

The Power of Classification

Here's another interesting one: the power of classification.

When putting toys away — "Cars go here, animals go here, blocks go here."

This is an experience of the mathematical concept of sets.

It might seem like nothing special to adults, but the act of sorting things based on a criterion is the gateway to sophisticated mathematical thinking.

Math Hidden in Everyday Life

There are opportunities when you go shopping, too.

"Can I have three apples, please?"

"Which one of these is bigger?"

"Do we have enough money?"

There's so much math hidden in everyday conversation.

Why STEAM, Not Just STEM

And this is the very heart of why it's STEAM and not just STEM.

Art and mathematics have a much deeper connection than you might think.

Take music, for example.

Rhythm is the repetition of patterns — it's mathematics itself.

Four-four time. Sixteenth notes. These are experiences of fractions.

When a child beats a drum — "tan, tan, ta-ta-tan" — keeping rhythm, that child is unconsciously experiencing mathematical patterns.

Drawing works the same way.

Symmetry, proportion, perspective. A picture is packed with mathematics.

Origami is also a wonderful mathematical experience.

Fold a piece of paper in half, and you get one-half.

Fold it in half again, and you get one-quarter.

In the process of folding an origami crane, children are feeling symmetry and proportion with their whole body.

Origami is celebrated in STEAM classrooms around the world.

In the United States, some schools have even incorporated it into their curriculum as "Origami Math."

When your child is building a symmetrical structure with blocks — that child is doing mathematics.

A much richer kind of mathematics than any calculation drill.

Math at Snack Time

At our school, even snack time becomes math time.

"We have ten cookies. If five people share them, how many does each person get?"

Just like that, children are naturally encountering the concept of division in the context of daily life.

Not calculations on paper — but learning math as their own lived experience using real objects.

Conclusion

Mathematical sense can only be developed through experience.

Rather than jumping straight into drills, first let your child have plenty of math experiences they can touch with their hands, see with their eyes, and feel with their body.

As children repeat the actions of sorting, lining up, and comparing with their own hands, the concept of numbers naturally soaks into their being.

Here's the takeaway.

The power of math is not the ability to do calculations on paper.

It's the ability to find patterns in the world and use them to create something new.

Rather than teaching that one plus one equals two, letting children share a snack in half — that experience of sharing builds a much richer mathematical sense.

Starting today, here's something I'd love for you to try: consciously use mathematical language in everyday life.

Big, small. Many, few. Long, short. Heavy, light.

Just weaving these comparison words into your conversations is enough to naturally awaken your child's math sensors.

The place where art and mathematics meet — that's where you'll find the most exciting part of STEAM education.

Please discover that excitement together with your child.

L9 Finland — Deep, Slow, and Joyful Learning (approx. 4 min)
Introduction

When you look at countries that lead the world in education, the way they incorporate STEAM education is completely different from one country to another.

The first country I want to introduce is Finland.

They have almost no homework, very few tests — and yet they consistently rank at the top of international academic assessments.

Why do you think that is?

Many parents feel the pressure to push their children harder academically, but Finland has achieved the world's best academic results with the exact opposite approach.

Phenomenon-Based Learning

The hallmark of Finnish education is a concept called phenomenon-based learning.

For example, they might spend an entire month researching the theme of "water."

From the science perspective, they study the three states of water. From the social studies perspective, they explore the issue of water resources.

And from the art perspective, they create music inspired by water.

They cross the boundaries between subjects and explore a single theme from multiple angles.

This is exactly what STEAM-based learning looks like.

A Culture Where Mistakes Are Welcome

What's especially wonderful about Finnish education is the culture where making mistakes is welcomed.

Even if a child says "I don't understand" to a teacher, there's no embarrassment.

In fact, realizing that you don't understand something is seen as an achievement.

This might feel very different from the education many of us grew up with, right?

In many educational settings, children feel ashamed when they make mistakes.

But in Finland, mistakes are naturally accepted as a normal part of the learning process.

Teachers as Facilitators

Another important point is that in Finland, teachers are not people who teach — they take the role of learning facilitators.

Rather than the teacher delivering information one-sidedly, their style is to support children as they make discoveries on their own.

The Essence We Can Learn from Finland

The essence we can take from Finland is: one theme, slowly and deeply.

For example, try making this week's theme "insects."

Observe insects at the park. Look them up in a reference book. Use tools to examine them.

Draw pictures of insects. Count the insects. Try building a little house for the insects.

Don't rush — follow your child's pace.

Instead of cramming in everything at once, take the time to savor one single theme.

That's where STEAM-based learning naturally emerges.

Protecting Time for Play

There's another important thing we can learn from Finnish education — and that is protecting time for play.

In Finland, there is always a fifteen-minute break between classes, and children go outside to play freely.

They never cut into this play time. Not ever.

Because they understand that play is the richest learning time of all.

The same is true at home.

Don't overschedule — make sure your child has time to play freely.

Try declaring, "Today is a day with no plans."

It's on days like those that a child's natural curiosity — that feeling of "I want to try this!" — rises up from within.

That is the at-home version of Finnish-style STEAM education.

Conclusion

Pick one theme and spend a whole week exploring it deeply.

I'm sure you and your child will make so many new discoveries together.

L10 Singapore — Teach Less, Learn More (approx. 4 min)
Introduction

Next up is Singapore.

This country consistently ranks at the top of international academic tests — and the national slogan they introduced in the 2000s was truly striking.

"Teach less, learn more."

Reduce the teaching, and increase the learning.

Most educational systems would push for more teaching, and yet — this top-performing country said to reduce the teaching.

That's a remarkable thing, isn't it?

From Rote Learning to Thinking

Singapore was originally a country built on rote learning.

It's a small nation with limited land and no natural resources — so they poured everything into education, because human talent was their greatest resource.

Test scores were high, but the education was centered on memorization.

Then they realized: just memorizing knowledge won't be enough for the era ahead.

So they intentionally reduced the amount that teachers teach, and reformed their education system to give children more time to think for themselves.

What the Reform Looked Like

Specifically, they introduced project-based learning.

They started giving problems that don't have just one right answer.

They increased class time devoted to team discussions.

What's fascinating about Singapore is that the government made a serious commitment to education that doesn't hand over the answers.

And did academic performance go down? Actually, it went up.

That's surprising, right?

They reduced the amount of teaching, yet academic performance improved.

Why? Because when children had more time to think for themselves, they moved beyond surface-level memorization to deeper, more genuine understanding.

Children who develop the ability to think can handle problems they've never seen before.

The 70/20/10 Rule of Learning

As I discussed in my book JOY-katsu, seventy percent of the learning that drives human growth comes from real-world experience.

What you figure out through your own thinking stays with you far more deeply than knowledge that's simply handed to you.

The Essence We Can Learn from Singapore

The essence we can take from Singapore is: have the courage not to teach too much.

Even when your child is struggling, don't give them the answer right away.

Ask, "What do you think you should do?"

That five seconds of holding back is what builds your child's thinking power.

Resist the urge to give the answer.

That is the at-home version of "Teach less, learn more."

Practical Examples

Here's what it looks like in practice. For example, when a puzzle isn't working out —

Instead of saying "It goes here," try offering just a hint: "What if you try turning it?"

When your child can't tie their shoelaces —

Instead of tying them for your child, try saying, "Take your time and give it a try," and just watch over them.

It takes time at first.

It can be frustrating, too.

But that waiting time is thinking time for your child.

The Joy of Learning Returns

By the way, as a result of this educational reform, Singapore reported that children developed not only stronger thinking skills, but also a greater ability to enjoy learning.

When the amount of teaching went down and children had more room to think for themselves, learning itself became fun.

That's exactly the essence of STEAM education.

The power of "not teaching" — proven at a national level by Singapore — is something you can absolutely put into practice at home.

It takes courage at first, but I promise you'll be amazed by your child's growth.

Conclusion

Not teaching too much does not mean neglecting your child.

It means creating room for your child to think on their own — and that is the greatest lesson we can learn from Singapore.

L11 America — Maker Education and the Culture of Celebrating Failure (approx. 4 min)
Introduction

Last is the country where I've lived for over twenty-six years — America.

Specifically, I want to talk about Silicon Valley.

Silicon Valley is the place where world-changing innovations are born — Google, Apple, Facebook, and more.

And there's a very unique culture here when it comes to education, too.

What Is Maker Education?

What's especially interesting about STEAM education in America is a movement called Maker Education.

A "Maker" means a person who makes things.

You learn through the experience of creating something with your own hands.

In Silicon Valley, there's an event called Maker Faire, where adults and children alike bring the things they've made and share them with each other.

Everything from robots to cardboard vending machines.

What's celebrated is not how polished the final product is — it's the act of making itself.

That is the Maker culture.

Fail Forward — Celebrating Failure

What's truly valued here is a culture that celebrates failure.

In Silicon Valley, failing at something is called "Fail Forward."

It means failure that moves you ahead.

When something doesn't work out, it means you tried — and that itself is progress.

In the startup world, when someone is asked, "How many times have you failed?" the person who can answer "Many times" is actually the one who earns more trust.

Because the number of failures equals the number of challenges you've taken on.

Failure as Learning — In Practice

At our school, we value this way of thinking deeply.

When a tower of blocks collapses —

Instead of "Oh, that's too bad," we say, "Whoa, it fell! Why do you think it fell?"

That's the moment when failure transforms into learning.

In many cultures, failure is met with "That didn't work out, did it?" But in the Maker culture, you say "Great try!"

That one phrase gives a child the courage to try again.

The Essence We Can Learn from America

The essence we can take from America is: enjoy the cycle of making, breaking, and making again.

Value the richness of the process over the beauty of the finished product.

Cardboard, plastic bottles, tape — whatever you have at home is more than enough.

When your child makes something and it doesn't quite work out, try asking: "Why do you think it broke? How could we make it work next time?" Think about it together.

That experience is what builds a heart that doesn't fear failure.

Praise the Process

And one more thing — when your child makes something, praise the process, not the result.

Instead of "Wow, what a great finished product!" try saying, "You put in so much thought, didn't you? You tried it over and over."

By praising the process, a child learns that the act of trying has value in and of itself.

Maker Day at Home

Try creating a Maker Day at home.

Even just once a month is great — a day where you use whatever's around the house to freely make something.

It's totally fine if nothing gets finished.

Making things is fun. Please savor that feeling together with your child.

Summary of Three Countries

Now, let's summarize what we've learned from these three countries.

Finland: deep and slow — explore one theme for a whole week.

Singapore: don't teach too much — before giving the answer, ask "What do you think?"

America: celebrate failure — "It broke! Why?" Think about it together.

What all three countries have in common is this: rather than teaching the answer, give children the experience of thinking.

Even across different cultures, the essence of STEAM education is the same.

L12 Why "Fun" Is the Best Fuel for Learning (approx. 5 min)
Introduction

So far, we've talked about the five elements of STEAM education and examples from countries around the world.

But there's one more very important thing we need to cover.

No matter how wonderful an educational method may be, it won't work if a child's heart is closed.

Have you ever had this experience?

You suggest something to your child — "This is really fun, let's try it!" — but your child isn't interested.

Even if you force them to do it, they can't focus at all.

So when does a child's heart open up?

It's when they feel safe and when they're having fun.

And this is where JOY practice and STEAM connect.

The Brain Science of Fun

There's some truly fascinating research in brain science.

When we're experiencing positive emotions, a neurotransmitter called dopamine is released in the brain.

This dopamine has two critically important effects.

Effect 1: Enhanced Memory

First, memory improves.

The hippocampus — the part of the brain responsible for memory — becomes activated, making it easier for what you've learned to become stored as long-term memory.

In other words, things you learn while having fun are hard to forget.

And on the flip side, things you did reluctantly are quickly forgotten.

I'm sure you've experienced this yourself, haven't you?

You've forgotten almost everything you learned in school classes, but the memories from a fun family trip are still vivid.

That's the power of dopamine.

Effect 2: Enhanced Curiosity

Second, curiosity increases.

The desire to know more, to do more — that natural drive to explore — wells up on its own.

Dopamine is sometimes called the "more" chemical in the brain.

Once dopamine is released, the brain craves more of that experience.

That's why children who've had a fun, exploratory experience want to do more of it on their own.

Fun Is the Engine of Learning

In other words, the feeling of "fun" is the engine of learning itself.

On the other hand, when a child is feeling anxiety or stress, a stress hormone called cortisol is released, and the brain's ability to learn decreases.

If you're yelling "Go study!" while forcing a child to study, the brain is actually in a state where it can't learn.

When a child has been yelled at, cortisol suppresses the function of the hippocampus, so no matter how long they study, it just won't sink in.

Stories from Our School

I've experienced this myself. The children at our school learn best when they're laughing during an experiment.

The baking soda experiment they did while squealing "Ahhh, look at all the bubbles!" and laughing hysterically — they still remember that months later.

But the content of a lesson they sat through after being scolded? They've already forgotten it by the next day.

This is exactly the difference between dopamine and cortisol.

Fun experiences come with dopamine, so they stick in memory. Boredom and tension come with cortisol, so they don't.

JOY Practice and the Dopamine Connection

Here's the key point.

Remember the JOY practice way of thinking.

First, the parent fills their own cup.

A sense of safety is born in the home.

The child feels secure enough to take on challenges.

This cycle makes perfect sense from a brain science perspective.

When a parent is frustrated, the child's brain gets cortisol. When a parent is calm, the child's brain gets dopamine.

The switch that turns on your child's curiosity is held by the parent's emotional state.

That's exactly why what I shared in my book JOY-katsu — that the parent's heart needs to be filled with warm joy first — is directly connected to the success of STEAM education.

You Don't Need to Try So Hard

You don't need to push yourself, thinking, "I have to provide a great education for my child."

First, just be calm and enjoy yourself as a parent.

That alone naturally creates a dopamine-rich environment in your child's brain.

When a parent is excited and curious, the child gets excited and curious too.

This emotional chain reaction is the most powerful driving force behind STEAM education.

The Opposite Is Also True

On the flip side, in homes where "Hurry up!" and "Do it properly!" are heard a lot, the child's brain is in a constant state of tension.

It becomes an environment where cortisol is easily released.

Of course, it's hard to be perfectly calm every single day.

But just being a little more mindful can make a real difference.

Please remember this: fun and a sense of safety are the best fuel for accelerating your child's learning.

L13 The Default Mode Network — Why "Doing Nothing" Sparks Creativity (approx. 5 min)
Introduction

I briefly touched on the DMN — the Default Mode Network — earlier in the course.

It turns out this is deeply connected to STEAM education.

Let me ask you — is your child's daily schedule packed full?

From morning to night, lessons and activities crammed in. Whenever there's a gap, do you feel like you need to fill it with something?

The truth is, it's in those very gaps that the most important skill in STEAM education is nurtured.

What Is the Default Mode Network?

As I also wrote in my book JOY-katsu, the brain has a neural circuit called the Default Mode Network — DMN.

The DMN is most active when you're spacing out.

Not when you're focused on something, but when your mind is wandering freely.

For example, when you're daydreaming in the bath. When you look up at the sky during a walk.

When you're gazing out the car window.

It looks like nothing is happening, right?

But inside the brain, something completely different is going on.

The Science Behind DMN

In 2024, a study published in the American medical journal Brain demonstrated that DMN activity is essential for creative thinking.

Joint research by the University of Utah and Baylor College of Medicine found that when part of the DMN was suppressed, creativity dropped significantly.

In other words, the time that looks like nothing is happening is actually what's running the brain's creative engine.

What the Brain Is Doing During Downtime

When the DMN is active, what the brain is doing is organizing past experiences, connecting pieces of information that were previously unrelated, and generating sudden flashes of insight.

That "Aha!" moment — when an idea pops into your head out of nowhere while you're doing nothing — that's thanks to the DMN.

The sparks of inspiration and fresh ideas that are so important in STEAM education are more likely to be born during idle moments than during focused concentration.

Newton's apple. Archimedes in the bath. Both ideas came to them while they were spacing out, right?

Don't Over-Schedule Your Child

Children are the same way. When you pack their schedule tight and keep them constantly focused on something, there's no time left for the DMN to do its work.

As I shared in my book JOY-katsu, children are born with an innate drive to discover.

For that drive to blossom, they need time when they're doing nothing.

Have the courage not to over-schedule. Leave some blank space.

The time when nothing is happening is the very margin that nurtures STEAM-style creativity.

JOY Practice and the Value of Spacing Out

In JOY practice, we value time spent spacing out.

Gazing outside right after waking up in the morning.

Spending quiet time before bed.

Settling your breathing while soaking in the tub.

These are directly building the foundation for STEAM education.

Even for adults, when time spent looking at your phone increases, time spent spacing out disappears.

When you're constantly taking in information, there's no room left for the DMN to work.

When parents themselves value their own time for spacing out, they become able to give their children permission to have time where they don't have to do anything.

The Courage to Leave Space

As I wrote in my book JOY-katsu, filling your child's schedule to the brim is not what love looks like.

Have the courage not to over-schedule.

The moment when your child thinks, "Huh? What's that? I kind of want to try it" — that is the doorway to the richest learning of all.

Great Minds and the Power of Doing Nothing

In fact, great scientists like Einstein and Darwin made daily walks part of their routine.

It's during the time when you're doing nothing that information gets organized inside your mind, and sudden bursts of inspiration are born. This is the DMN at work.

When your child is outside, just staring off into space, you might feel the urge to say, "What are you doing? Go play!"

But that might be exactly the time when creativity is being cultivated inside their brain.

Please just let them be.

Conclusion

JOY practice and STEAM education are deeply connected in that they both cherish the value of open space.

L14 Emotional Co-Regulation — Why a Calm Parent Is the Best STEAM Teacher (approx. 6 min)
Introduction

Finally, let me share one more scientific concept that connects JOY practice and STEAM.

That is emotional co-regulation.

It's a bit of a technical term, but in simple words, it's the brain's mechanism by which a parent's emotional state is directly transmitted to their child.

What Is Emotional Co-Regulation?

Emotional co-regulation, put simply, means this: children aren't yet able to control their emotions on their own.

So they borrow the emotional state of the adult nearby to calm themselves down. That's how the brain works.

When a baby is crying and the mother holds them gently and says "It's okay" in a calm voice, the baby settles down.

This isn't just soothing. The calm rhythm of the mother's nervous system is actually being transmitted to the baby's nervous system.

The concept of mirror neurons that we discussed when talking about the SMILE method is also at work here.

Emotional Contagion

As I also touched on in my book JOY-katsu, there's a phenomenon called emotional contagion that's closely related to this.

Through a parent's facial expressions, tone of voice, and gestures, emotions are unconsciously transmitted to the child.

Especially in close relationships like the parent-child bond, this effect shows up very strongly.

How Co-Regulation Connects to STEAM Education

So how does this mechanism of co-regulation relate to STEAM education?

Imagine a moment when your child tries something and fails.

Their tower of blocks collapsed. An experiment didn't work out.

The picture they drew didn't turn out the way they wanted.

In that moment, the state of the parent sitting beside them changes how the child responds.

If the parent is frustrated, the child learns that failure equals something scary. And they start avoiding challenges.

If the parent is calm, the child feels that failure equals something that's okay. And they can try again.

This is a huge difference.

Why a Safe Environment Matters

In STEAM education, learning from failure is extremely important. But without a safe environment when failure happens, children will become afraid of failure and stop taking on challenges.

In other words, the parent being in a state of warmth and fulfillment is itself what accelerates the child's STEAM-based exploration.

Fill Your Own Cup First

What we learned in my book JOY-katsu — that the parent must tend to themselves first — becomes the key here as well.

When a parent is spending time in a calm, relaxed atmosphere, the child feels "It's okay" and feels secure enough to take on challenges.

A parent's smile flips the switch on a child's curiosity.

And the reverse is also true — when a parent is tired or carrying anxiety, the child picks up on it sensitively, and their motivation to try new things drops.

When a child senses "Something feels different about Mommy today," they unconsciously go into self-protection mode, prioritizing safety over exploration.

The Oxygen Mask Metaphor

That's exactly why the airplane oxygen mask metaphor — which I also shared in my book JOY-katsu — is so important. Put on your own oxygen mask first, before helping your child with theirs.

If you want to nurture your child's exploratory drive, the prerequisite is that you, the parent, are breathing in plenty of heart-oxygen first.

Beyond Words

Co-regulation doesn't travel through words.

Even if you're saying "It's okay" with your words, if your face is tense, your child will believe the expression, not the words.

That's why what truly matters is the parent being genuinely calm from the heart. And that is what JOY practice is for.

When you're doing an experiment together with your child and you're genuinely enjoying it — when your face shows that you truly find it fascinating — safety and curiosity are transmitted to your child's brain.

But if you're feeling rushed, thinking "I need to clean this up" — that sense of urgency gets transmitted too. The power of co-regulation is that strong.

JOY Practice and STEAM: The Full Picture

Let me summarize the relationship between JOY practice and STEAM education.

The parent fills their own cup.

This creates a dopamine-rich environment for the child.

Time for spacing out — the DMN nurtures creativity.

A calm emotional state — through co-regulation, it creates the soil where challenges are welcomed.

The most powerful teaching tool for STEAM education is a calm, fulfilled parent.

That is the perspective that Reiko-sensei's JOY practice brings to STEAM education — a perspective unlike anything else in the world.

L15 STEAM Play for Ages Zero to Six (approx. 4 min)
Introduction

All right, it's time for the hands-on section!

From here, I'm going to introduce STEAM and JOY practice play activities you can do at home with children ages zero to six.

What's important is this: you don't need any special tools or materials at all.

No matter where you are in the world, you can start with what you already have at home.

Let me share the most important point first.

It's this: have fun together with your child.

Don't forget the JOY practice perspective.

Not "for your child" — but enjoying it alongside them.

That attitude is the secret to making STEAM education work.

Ages Zero to Three — Water Play and Science

Let me give you a classic example for ages zero to three.

Water play — and science.

This age is the period of sensory exploration.

The five senses are the antennae that feel the world before thinking even begins.

Giving your child plenty of sensory stimulation during this stage builds the foundation for STEAM education.

Let me introduce one go-to activity.

Fill a bathtub or basin with water and try floating all sorts of things in it.

A spoon sinks. But a bowl floats. Why is that?

Even at zero years old, babies will show you a look of amazement at things floating and sinking.

To take the play a step further, fill a plastic bottle with water and poke some holes in it.

Change the position of the holes, and the way the water comes out changes.

Your child will be absolutely thrilled.

This is actually an experiment in water pressure!

Reducing "No" — Turning Mistakes into Learning

The most important thing at this age is to reduce the number of times you say "no."

Your child spills water from a cup onto the table.

Your first instinct is to say "Don't do that!" right?

But instead, if you just say, "Oh, the water spilled! How does the water move on the table?" — that one shift turns a "no" into a learning moment.

Ages Three to Six — Kitchen Experiments and Science

Now, a classic example for ages three to six.

Kitchen experiments — and science.

Once children pass age three, the storm of "why?" really begins.

This stage is the golden age of curiosity.

My recommendation is kitchen experiments.

Pour vinegar on baking soda and bubbles appear.

The key is not to tell them what will happen.

Ask, "What do you think will happen when we pour vinegar on the baking soda?" — and then try it together.

The look on your child's face when the fizzy bubbles appear —

That is what makes STEAM education so exciting.

More Activities and the Workbook

Beyond that, there are so many activities — cardboard crafts, drawing, nature observation, homemade instruments, and more.

I've compiled all the activities by age group in the STEAM Play Workbook that comes with this lecture.

Please download it, print it out, and keep it nearby.

It includes a checklist, so as you try each activity with your child, go ahead and check them off together.

Summary

Let me wrap up.

Ages zero to three: sensory exploration. Share in the wonder by saying, "That's so interesting!"

Ages three to six: exploring "why." Spark their thinking by asking, "What do you think?"

What's true across every age is this: rather than giving them the answer, give them the time to think and a safe space to do it in.

That is the heart of JOY practice and STEAM combined.

You don't have to do STEAM play every day.

Even once a week is great — the most important thing is keeping a pace that's easy and enjoyable, doing it whenever you feel like it.

Use the workbook as your guide, and please — start enjoying STEAM with your child today.

L16 How SMILE and STEAM Connect (approx. 6 min)
Introduction

The SMILE framework — the five non-cognitive skills we explored in our course on non-cognitive skills.

STEAM education is actually completely connected to SMILE.

Let's take a clear look at how the SMILE framework and STEAM education connect.

Let's go through them one by one.

S — Social Skills and STEAM

S: Social Skills and STEAM.

STEAM-style exploration becomes even more powerful when done together with friends.

Experimenting together. Creating something together.

The power of collaboration grows naturally.

For example, at our school, the children build cardboard houses as a team.

"Let's put a window here!" "The door should go over there!" — they work it out through discussion.

Sometimes their ideas clash, but through that process, they develop the ability to find a middle ground: "Well, how about we try this?"

M — Manners and STEAM

M: Manners and STEAM.

Experiments have procedures, and tools have proper ways of being handled.

Put things back when you're done.

Treat your friends' creations with care.

Within STEAM activities, manners develop naturally.

How to use scissors. Putting the cap back on the glue.

Returning tools to where they belong.

These are things children pick up naturally through repeated STEAM activities — without adults having to say "Do it!"

I — Interest and STEAM

I: Interest and STEAM.

This is the most direct connection of all.

"Why?" "I want to try!" — that curiosity is the very engine that drives STEAM education.

Cherishing a child's desire to do something and watching over their free thinking —

That is how you nurture Interest, and it's also the starting point of STEAM education.

L — Language and STEAM

L: Language and STEAM.

Putting your discoveries into words.

"This is what happened." "The reason is because..."

STEAM education strengthens expressive ability and communication skills at the same time.

It's not just about any one language — it's the ability to convey your thoughts to someone else. That's what grows here.

When a child reports, "Mommy, look! This is what happened!" —

That moment contains a sophisticated language activity: observing facts, organizing them into words, and communicating them clearly to another person.

E — Enthusiasm and STEAM

E: Enthusiasm and STEAM.

The power to say "One more time!" even when things don't work out.

The strength to dive in without fearing failure and to keep going until you're done.

The trial-and-error process of STEAM is exactly what builds this power.

The E in SMILE — as we covered in JOY-katsu — is the inner strength to become fully absorbed in something you've chosen and to see it through without giving up.

The STEAM experience of "Let me try building it one more time" naturally strengthens this very ability.

The Relationship Between SMILE, STEAM, and JOY Practice

Here's the key point.

SMILE and STEAM — and the foundation that supports them both is JOY practice.

SMILE: the skills you want to nurture.

STEAM: how you nurture them.

JOY practice: the environment in which they grow.

When all three come together, children grow in their own unique way, with real strength.

Let me explain this relationship using the image of a tree.

JOY practice is the soil.

Without warm, nourishing soil, nothing can grow.

STEAM is the water and sunlight.

The stimulation of exploratory experiences fuels growth.

And SMILE is the flowers and fruit — the five non-cognitive skills that emerge as the result of that growth.

Everything Is Connected

In other words, the SMILE framework we explored in our course on non-cognitive skills, the STEAM approach we've been learning in this course, and JOY practice — these three are not separate things. They are all connected within one larger educational philosophy.

What matters is not stressing yourself out thinking, "I have to do all of this."

If you enjoy STEAM-style play in your everyday life, the five skills of SMILE will develop naturally.

And the foundation beneath it all is the emotional stability you've built through JOY practice.

When you see your child completely absorbed in something, try observing: "Which SMILE skill is growing right now?"

I'm sure you'll notice that within a single play experience, multiple skills are being nurtured at the same time.

Please remember this simple cycle.

In the next lecture, I'll share a final summary and message for this course.

L17 Summary and Final Message (approx. 6 min)
What I Most Wanted to Share

What I most wanted to share with you in this course is this:

STEAM is not a special kind of education for special children.

It's a way of thinking that protects the sense of wonder every child is born with — and makes sure it isn't crushed.

Reflecting on Your Journey

Throughout this course, has anything shifted inside you?

If you started out thinking, "STEAM education sounds so difficult," and now you're feeling, "Oh, it's actually closer to everyday life than I thought" — that makes me so happy.

Course Summary

Let me summarize everything we've covered in this course.

Let's look back at what we learned.

Section 1.

The essence of STEAM education is the cycle of inquiry.

Section 2.

The neuroscience that validates the power of exploratory experiences.

Synapse formation. Executive function.

Section 3.

S, T, and E are already present in everyday life.

Section 4.

A and M are deeply connected to each other.

Section 5.

Learning from the world's most education-forward countries — education that makes children think.

Section 6.

The scientific mechanism through which JOY accelerates STEAM.

Dopamine, the default mode network, and empathy.

Section 7.

A practical guide for ages zero to six.

Three Things You Can Start Today

Finally, here are three things you can start today.

Number one. When your child asks "Why?" — respond with "What do you think?"

Before giving the answer, give your child five seconds to think for themselves.

When they ask "Why?" — before reaching for your phone, try asking, "What do you think, sweetie?"

It's just one phrase, but that single question awakens the little scientist inside your child.

Number two. Don't take away their time to zone out.

Don't fill up every minute of their schedule.

Doing nothing is what develops the default mode network.

Look at today's schedule and try to carve out just one block of time where nothing is planned.

That empty space becomes the seedbed of your child's creativity.

Number three. First, fill yourself with JOY.

Your smile is what flips the switch for safety and curiosity inside your child's brain.

Today, make just a little time for yourself.

Slowly savor your favorite cup of coffee.

Listen to some music you love.

Just that is enough to change the atmosphere in your home.

You Don't Need to Be an Expert

Have you ever thought something like this?

"I'm not an expert — I'm not sure I can give my child a proper education."

It's okay.

What STEAM education requires is not specialized knowledge.

It's the ability to wonder together.

When your child says, "Why is that?" — all you have to do is say, "You're right. Why is that?" and tilt your head together.

That alone lights the fire of inquiry inside your child.

The Greatest Gift

What I most wanted to convey in my book JOY-katsu is this:

The greatest gift of parenting is raising a child who can find joy on their own.

STEAM education is exactly the same.

The gift isn't giving them the answer.

It's letting them experience the joy of thinking for themselves and making their own discoveries.

That is the greatest STEAM education a parent can give.

If this course has sparked even a small feeling in you of "Oh, so that's what STEAM education really is" or "I think we could do this at home too" — I'm truly happy.

Small Steps, Big Change

You don't have to do it perfectly.

You don't have to do it every day.

Once a week is enough.

Whenever you feel like it, just tilt your head together with your child and wonder, "Why is that?"

That's more than enough.

What you do today won't show visible results tomorrow.

But six months from now, a year from now, when you see your child thinking and acting on their own —

You'll know: all those small moments added up and bore fruit.

Small steps, accumulated over time, eventually create great change.

Being there with a smile.

Being there to share in the surprise.

Watching over them and saying "It's okay" when they stumble.

That is the finest STEAM education there is — one that holds true no matter where in the world you go.

Closing

Please, starting today, enjoy STEAM and JOY together with your child.

I hope to see you again in another course!

Thank you so much.
